## Page 1


CANONICAL EXTENSIONS OF p-ADIC SHTUKAS ON TOROIDAL
COMPACTIFICATIONS OF SHIMURA VARIETIES
SHENGKAI MAO AND PEIHANG WU
Abstract. We construct canonical extensions of p-adic shtukas on integral models of toroidal com-
pactifications of abelian-type Shimura varieties with quasi-parahoric levels at any prime number p.
More precisely, we define the notion of a log diamond as a v-sheaf associated with a log scheme
over Zp and construct a p-adic log shtuka over the log diamond of an integral toroidal compact-
ification of an abelian-type Shimura variety by studying the “degeneration” of the shtuka at the
boundary. Moreover, we provide a definition of canonical integral models of toroidal and minimal
compactifications in the sense of Pappas and Rapoport, and verify it in the same generality as
above.
Applications include the canonicity and functoriality of integral toroidal compactifications, as
well as an axiomatic proof of the well-positionedness of all well-known stratifications on the special
fiber.

Introduction
Let (G, X) be a Shimura datum with reflex field E(G, X). Denote by {ShK(G, X)}K⊂G(Af),neat
the associated Shimura varieties. Fix a prime number p and a place v|p of E.
In [PR24], building on Scholze’s idea and theory of p-adic shtukas as substitutes for “motives”
over p-adic fields, Pappas and Rapoport developed a general theory of (p-adic) shtukas and applied
it to local and global Shimura varieties. They constructed canonical integral models over Spec OEv,
proving their uniqueness and functoriality, and treated Hodge-type Shimura varieties with stabilizer
parahoric level. This was extended to general parahoric levels in [DvHKZ26], and to abelian-type
Shimura varieties in [DY25] using the Kisin–Pappas–Zhou models [KPZ24].
Toroidal compactifications of Shimura varieties over the complex numbers were constructed in
[AMRT10], and for mixed Shimura varieties over reflex fields in [Pin90]. For a cone decomposition
Σ, the compactification ShΣ
K(G, X) has boundary charts described by certain toric embeddings of
mixed Shimura varieties indexed by cusp labels [(Φ, σ)] ∈CuspK(G, X, Σ).
Integral versions of this picture were developed in [FC90], [Lan13], [MP19], and [Wu25]. In the
PEL-type setting, boundary strata and their gluings are described in terms of degenerations of
abelian schemes. For Shimura varieties of Hodge type and of abelian type, the strategy is different:
one first obtains candidate integral models from integral toroidal compactifications of Siegel type
via normalizations and passing to quotients, and the main technical difficulties arise in verifying
that the boundary components of these models satisfy the required structural properties.
A natural question thus arises:
Q: If one interprets the integral models constructed in the framework of [PR24] as moduli spaces
of shtukas, then for suitably defined integral models of their toroidal compactifications, one should,
at least in principle, be able to provide a precise description of their degeneration behavior along
the boundary.
In this paper, we discuss our understanding of this question. The goals of this paper are threefold.
(1) We develop a theory of log shtukas over log diamonds over Spd Zp, extending the theory of
shtukas over diamonds in [PR24]. On the generic fiber, log shtukas correspond to certain
pro-Kummer étale local systems with Hodge–Tate period maps, while on the special fiber,
they recover the usual shtukas. We also prove the rigidity of extensions from the generic
fiber to the integral base.
(2) We construct log shtukas on toroidal compactifications of integral models of abelian-type
Shimura varieties with quasi-parahoric level (which slightly generalizes Kisin–Pappas–Zhou
models), and provide an axiomatic framework that is applicable to general Shimura vari-
eties, in which the extension of shtukas to toroidal compactifications does not rely on the
theory of abelian schemes or p-divisible groups. Hence, we expect that this picture should
also hold in general, although canonical integral models have not yet been constructed for
general parahoric groups at any p. In particular, this allows us to prove the uniqueness and
functoriality of toroidal compactifications of integral models, including compatibility with
changes of parahoric levels, generalizing results in [Wu25] and [Mao25a].


## Page 3


3
(3) We provide a description of how the shtukas supported on integral models degenerate
along the boundary.
Along the way, we show that various stratifications on the spe-
cial fibers, including central leaves, Newton strata, Kottwitz–Rapoport (KR) strata, and
Ekedahl–Kottwitz–Oort–Rapoport (EKOR) strata defined using shtukas have good proper-
ties along the boundary. This reproves and generalizes some results in [Box15], [LS18a] and
[Mao25b].
Log shtukas on log diamonds. Let E be a local field of characteristic 0, let G be a reductive group
over Qp, let µ be a minuscule cocharacter, and let G be a parahoric group scheme of G over Zp.
Recall that, in [PR24], a theory of shtukas over diamonds was developed. Given a scheme X
that is separated, of finite type over Zp, there are different ways to attach certain diamonds to X,
e.g., X♢, X⋄, and X♢/ (see [AGLR22, §2.2] and [PR24, §2.1]). One can define a family of shtukas
(P, ϕP) on X♢(with one leg bounded by µ) as a 1-morphism between v-stacks X♢→ShtG,µ. For
a locally noetherian adic space X over Spa(E, OE), the authors proved that there is an equivalence
between G-shtukas over X♢and pairs (P, H) consisting of pro-étale G(Zp)-torsors P over X♢and
Hodge-Tate maps H (see [PR24, Prop. 2.5.3]). An important feature is that, given X a separated
normal scheme of finite type over OE, a morphism of G-shtukas on the generic fiber (Xη)♢extends
uniquely over the integral base X ♢(see [PR24, Thm. 2.7.7 and Cor. 2.7.9]). In characteristic p,
shtukas are crystalline in nature (see [PR24, Thm. 2.3.8 and Ex. 2.4.9]).
To define the extension of shtukas on toroidal compactifications, we need to generalize the theory
of diamonds and shtukas to the logarithmic setting. Let (X, MX) be a locally Noetherian fs log
adic space over Spa(Zp).

Let (G, X) be an abelian-type Shimura datum, let p be any prime, and let K = KpKp be any neat
level. By a series of works [FC90], [Lan16], [MP19], [Wu25], and others, there exist good toroidal
and minimal compactifications of integral models satisfying Axiom 5.1 (see Theorem 5.5).
On the other hand, in [PR24], [DvHKZ26], and [DY25], when Kp is parahoric, a conjectural
framework of canonical integral models of the pro-system {ShKpKp(G, X)}Kp⊂G(Ap

In order to define and construct canonical integral models of toroidal compactifications, we com-
bine the two above-mentioned aspects and study the canonical integral models of boundary mixed
Shimura varieties. For a mixed Shimura datum (P, X), P is a non-reductive group. We generalize
the notions of local models and shtukas with one leg bounded by µ to non-reductive groups, and
prove many functoriality results that are used in later sections. The definitions and propositions
are parallel to those defined using reductive groups.

Remark. We remark that, even for compact Shimura varieties, the first item already extends the
known cases in the literature (see [PR24], [DvHKZ26] and [DY25]) to cases allowing all primes and
all quasi-parahoric levels for all abelian-type Shimura data. In [DvHKZ26], the authors showed the
first item of Theorem D in the Hodge-type case for any p and any quasi-parahoric Kp; in [DY25],
the authors proved the result in the abelian-type case when p > 2 and Kp is parahoric.
In fact, even if one just wants to generalize these results to compactifications, it is still much
more convenient to work with Bruhat-Tits stabilizer levels than parahoric levels. This fact forced us
to prove the theorem in the above generality.
Remark (See also Remark 7.25). To show the theorem above, it is crucial for us to handle non-R-
smooth (cf. [KPZ24, 2.1.4] for the definition of R-smoothness) Shimura data (G, X) with parahoric

Our method here provides a description of these extensions at boundaries, which will be given below
in more detail; and an important advantage is that the extension step only uses (log) Gc-shtukas,
which makes it possible to be applied to cases beyond abelian-type Shimura varieties (see Theorem
5.19 and Lemma 5.15).

In [Box15] and [LS18a], the authors formulate the notion of well-positioned subschemes. Well-
positioned strata have good (partial) toroidal and minimal compactifications along the boundary,
with many good qualitative properties described in Axiom 5.1, as if they were Shimura varieties
in characteristic 0. It was proved in [LS18a] that, for PEL-type Shimura varieties, Newton strata,
central leaves, KR strata, and EKOR strata are well positioned. These results were generalized to
the Hodge-type case in [Mao25b], where it was shown that their partial minimal compactifications
are again of the same type of strata (e.g., the boundary of the partial minimal compactification of a
Newton stratum is stratified by Newton strata on the boundary). The proofs in [LS18a] essentially
used the degeneration of p-divisible groups along the boundary. Under the degeneration diagram
(0.1) (cf.
Proposition 5.34), and since the shtukas in characteristic p are crystalline in nature,
we find that the well-positioned property of all these strata can be naturally explained using the
degeneration of shtukas.
Assume there exist canonical integral models {S Σ
K}Kp and {S min
K
}Kp in the sense of Definition
5.27 (for example, in the setting of Theorem D).
Theorem F.
(1) Newton strata (resp. central leaves, KR strata, and EKOR strata), their connected com-
ponents, and their closures are well positioned locally closed subschemes in the sense of
Definition 7.1.
(2) The boundary of the partial minimal compactifications of Newton strata (resp. central leaves,
KR strata) is stratified by Newton strata (resp. central leaves, KR strata) on the boundary;
see Proposition 7.7, 7.12, and 7.19 for details.
Besides generalizing the well-positioned property and the boundary descriptions of partial min-
imal compactifications from the Hodge-type case to a more general framework (at least to the
abelian-type case), we also provide a new description of partial toroidal compactifications of these
strata.
Proposition (Proposition 7.13, 7.14, 7.21, 7.26). The partial toroidal compactifications of Newton
strata (resp. central leaves, KR strata, and EKOR strata) coincide with the Newton strata (resp.
central leaves, KR strata, and EKOR strata) defined using S Σ
K(G, X)perf

Gc,µc,δ=1.
Remark. In a recent paper [Ino25], Kentaro Inoue constructed a log prismatic realization in the
Hodge-type case for smooth integral models of toroidal compactifications by developing a log prismatic
Dieudonné theory using log p-divisible groups. Combining with it, the method provided here can be


## Page 8


8
extended to the log prismatic context for more general (G, X), and may also give the log prismatic
realizations finer boundary descriptions. In fact, we can construct a realization functor from log
prismatic F-crystals to log shtukas that is compatible with the étale realization functor. Together
with Inoue, we plan to explore this direction.
Structure of the article. Section 1 can be viewed as a preliminary section of this paper, where we
start by generalizing the definition of some objects in [SW20] to non-reductive groups and proving
basic properties. In Section 2, we define and study log diamonds and shtukas, generalizing and
modifying the previous work [PR24] and [KY25]. Some results analogous to [PR24, Sec. 2] will be
shown in the log setting. In Section 3, we study the p-adic local systems on mixed Shimura varieties.
In Section 4, we define canonical integral models for boundary mixed Shimura varieties. Some basic
properties of these canonical integral models will be proved. In Section 5, we show the existence
of canonical extensions of shtukas (or rather, log shtukas) in an axiomatic setup. We also define
canonical integral models for toroidal/minimal compactifications. In Section 6, we demonstrate that
canonical integral models for compactifications exist for all abelian-type Shimura data, all primes,
and all quasi-parahoric levels. In particular, the canonical integral models for (boundary mixed)
Shimura varieties also exist in the same generality. In Section 7, we prove the well-positionedness
of all well-known stratifications of special fibers of Shimura varieties under the axioms in Section 5.
Here is a workflow diagram:
§2
§5
§7
§1
§3
§4
§6 .

Notation and conventions. Fix a prime number p > 0. All monoids are commutative. All rings have
identities. For the conventions in p-adic geometry (and perfectoid geometry), we follow [SW20] and
[Sch26]. For the definition of an adic space being locally Noetherian, we follow [DLLZ23a, p.4].
For conventions on (mixed) Shimura varieties and compactifications, we mainly follow [Wu25] (we
refer readers to the latest version here, which will be updated on arXiv in the future). For the
conventions in log geometry, we mainly follow [Kat89], [Ogu18], and [DLLZ23a]. A constant sheaf
of monoids on X with values in P is denoted by PX instead of P to avoid awkward conventions
such as “Pa”. Log structures are written multiplicatively, but charts are written additively. For a
Shimura datum (G, X), G is a reductive group over Q. We also write G⊗Q Qp as G when it is clear
in the context. There are several “E” in different fonts: In general, “E” denotes global fields; “E”
denotes local fields; “E” denotes tori. For a smooth affine group scheme G over Zp, we usually denote
Kp = G(Zp) and ˘Kp = G(˘Zp). Here ˘
(∗) means the set of ˘Zp-points. Let Perf denote the category
of perfectoid spaces of characteristic p, and Perfd denote the category of all perfectoid spaces. Let
PCAlgop denote the category of affine perfect schemes. Some Galois groups: Γ = Gal(Qp|Qp),
I = Gal(Qp|˘Qp), Σ0 = ⟨σ⟩= Gal(˘Qp|Qp).
The group-theoretic conventions in §1 and a few lemmas and definitions in §3 and §4 are slightly
different from other places in the article. In §1, G usually denotes the Levi quotient of a linear
algebraic group P, while an embedding of P into a reductive group is denoted by P ,→G′. In other
places, G usually denotes the group that P (or rather, PΦ) maps into.


## Page 9

1. Shtukas for non-reductive groups
To understand the geometry of toroidal compactifications of Shimura varieties, we study mixed
Shimura varieties. As a first step, we discuss local models and shtukas for non-reductive groups in
detail. Many properties are analogous to those for reductive groups; consequently, we present only
the properties needed in later sections.

dR-affine Grassmannian.

1.1.2. Diamond functors. For a scheme X over Zp, there are three different notions of diamonds
associated with X. See [AGLR22, §2.2] and [PR24, §2.1].
• “big diamond” X♢: A v-sheaf X♢sending any affinoid perfectoid S ∈Ob Perf to the iso-
morphism classes of tuples (S♯, ι, f), where (S♯, ι) is an untilt and (f : S♯→X) ∈X(S♯).
• “small diamond” X⋄: A v-sheaf X⋄sending any affinoid perfectoid S = Spa(A, A+) ∈
Ob Perf to the isomorphism classes of tuples (S♯= Spa(A♯, A♯,+), ι, f), where (S♯, ι) is an
untilt and f ∈X(A♯,+).
• “slashed diamond” X♢/ := X⋄`
X⋄×Spd ZpSpd Qp(XQp)♢.
If X is proper over Zp, the three notions above are the same by the valuative criterion of proper-
ness.
For a pre-adic space X over Zp, the similarly-defined functor X♢sending S ∈Ob Perf to (S♯, ι, f :
S♯→X) is also a v-sheaf over Perf by [SW20, Lem. 18.1.1].

Definition 1.11.
(1) We say µ is quotient-minuscule in P if µ is minuscule in the reductive quotient G.
(2) We say µ is minuscule in P if the weights of µ on Lie P are {−1, 0, 1}.
It follows from the definition that µ is minuscule implies that µ is quotient-minuscule.

Definition 1.13. We say that (P, µ) comes from the boundary if there exists a reductive Qp-
group G′ with a parabolic Qp-subgroup Q ⊂G′ such that
(1) µ : Gm
µ→P →G′ is a minuscule cocharacter in G′ defined over a field extension of E,
(2) {Uα| ⟨µ, α⟩> 0, α ∈Φ′} ⊂Q, where Φ′ is the absolute root datum of G′.
(3) P is a normal subgroup of Q with RuP = RuQ.
In this case, we denote the Levi quotient of Q by L.

conventions in [Pin90, §1.3]). In particular, if ⟨µx′, α⟩> 0, then the weight of Lie Uα under hx′ ◦w
has to be at least 0 by (1.2). Hence Lie Uα ⊂(Lie Q)C, since (Lie Q)C is the direct sum of all
nonnegative weight spaces in (Lie G′)C under the adjoint action of hx′ ◦w; see [Pin90, 4.1 and Prop.
4.6].
□
Lemma 1.15. If (P, µ) comes from the boundary, then the immersions FlP,µ →FlQ,µ →FlG′,µ
are open immersions; in particular, they have the same dimension.
Proof. This follows from a calculation of the tangent spaces at the identity. Since the construction
of Fl is compatible with base change, we base change everything to an algebraically closed field.
We take a maximal torus T inside Q,
Lie G/ Lie Gµ = ⊕α∈Φ,⟨µ,α⟩>0 Lie Uα = Lie Q/ Lie Qµ.
Let us denote W := RuP = RuQ, Gh := P/W, L := Q/W, and Gl := Q/P. We have an exact
sequence of reductive groups 1 →Gh →L →Gl →1, and the root data of Gh and Gl are
orthogonal. In particular, Lie L/ Lie Lµ = Lie Gh/ Lie Gh,µ; thus Lie Q/ Lie Qµ = Lie P/ Lie Pµ.
□
Remark 1.16. Under the setting of Lemma 1.14, let us give a more geometric explanation of
Lemma 1.15. Consider the map τ : X →π0(X) × Hom(SC, PC) that sends x to ([x], ωx ◦h∞). Let

orbit containing τ(x) for some x ∈X. Let ϕ : D →ϕ(D) ⊂Hom(SC, PC) be the natural projection
with finite fibers. Fix h ∈ϕ(D), let C(h) be the centralizer of h in P(R)W(C). Let M be a faithful
representation of P. By the proof of [Pin90, Prop. 1.7], we have
ϕ(D) = P(R)W(C)/C(h) ,→P(C)/exp(F 0(Lie P)C) = P(C)/Pµ(C) ,→Grass(M)(C),
where the first morphism is an open embedding, and the last morphism is the closed embedding given
by the Hodge filtration associated with the Hodge cocharacter µ determined by h. In particular, the
dimension of D agrees with that of FlP,µ as complex manifolds. This is analogous to the Borel
embedding X′ ,→˜X′ = G′/G′
µ′ = FlG′,µ′, which also shows that the dimension of X′ equals that of
FlG′,µ′. Let X′ ⊂X be the preimage of D under τ, which is a union of connected components of
X. Then X′ →D is an open embedding; see [Pin90, Prop. 4.15(a)]. Thus X′ and D have the same
dimension. Finally, since µ and µ′ are conjugate in G′, we conclude that FlP,µ and FlG′,µ have the
same dimension.

2. Log diamonds and log shtukas
We discuss some theorems in [PR24, Sec. 2] in the log setting. To start with, we also introduce
some basic results about log diamonds defined in the v-topology for log adic spaces, schemes and
formal schemes. Since we will deal with many slightly different cases simultaneously, the readers
will be frequently reminded about the cases under consideration before seeing the results.
2.1. Log diamonds associated with log schemes. Our goal is to develop results on log diamonds
associated with fs log schemes over Zp. This may be viewed as a common generalization of some
definitions and propositions in [SW20, §18] and [KY25, §7].
2.1.1.
Let X be a scheme locally of finite type over Zp. We write Xad as the adic space that
represents the fiber product
X ×Spec Zp Spa Zp.
Let b
X be the formal scheme defined by the p-adic completion of X and b
Xad the adic space associated
with it. We can also define Xad similarly when X is of finite type over Qp.
When X is separated and of finite type, there is an open embedding b
Xad ,→Xad. Now let X be
a separated scheme of finite type over OE, where E is a finite field extension of Qp. We list some
easy facts that will be used later:
(1) Let Xη be the generic fiber of X, we can also define (Xη)ad = Xη ×Spec E Spa(E, OE) in the
sense of [Hub94, Proposition 3.8], then
(2.1)
(Xη)ad = (X ×Spec OE Spa OE) ×Spa OE Spa(E, OE) = (Xad)η.
(2) Let X →Y ←Z be schemes separated locally of finite type over E, then Xad, Y ad, Zad
are Tate. In particular, Xad →Y ad ←Zad are adic, the fiber product exists. Checking the
functoriality in [Hub94, Proposition 3.8], we have
(2.2)
(X ×Y Z)ad = Xad ×Y ad Zad
(3) Let X be a smooth variety over E, then Xad is a sousperfectoid analytic adic space.
(4) Let b
X →bY ←bZ be formal schemes separated locally of finite type over OE, then (2.2) also
holds:
(2.3)
( b
X ×bY bZ)ad = b
Xad ×bY ad bZad.
(5) The functor X♢(resp. X⋄) can be constructed via taking the diamond functor for adic spaces
for Xad (resp.
b
Xad); both (−)♢and (−)⋄commute with fiber products in the category of
separated schemes of finite type over OE, and are compatible with (2.2) and (2.3) under
(2.1).
2.1.2.
Let us recall some terminology in log geometry. For details, we refer the readers to [Kat89],
[Ogu18], [DLLZ23a] and [KY25].
Let P be a monoid.
One can associate a group Pgp with a natural homomorphism between
monoids gpP : P →Pgp; in fact, P 7→Pgp is the left adjoint functor of the natural inclusion of the
category of groups into the category of monoids.
We say that P is integral if gpP is injective, say that P is saturated, if P is integral and any
x ∈Pgp such that nx ∈P for some n ∈Z>0 is in P, and say that P is fine if P is integral and finitely
generated. We say that P is fs if P is both fine and saturated. Denote P := P/P×.
We say an adic space X is étale sheafy if Xét is a site and if OXét is a sheaf. This is satisfied, for
example, when X is analytic stably sheafy or is Noetherian affinoid (cf. [DLLZ23a, Cor. A.11]).


## Page 27


27
Definition 2.1 ([DLLZ23a, Def. 2.2.2]). Let X be an étale sheafy adic space. A prelog structure on
X is a pair (MX, α), where MX is a sheaf of monoids over Xét and α : MX →OXét is a morphism
of sheaves of monoids, and such a pair is a log structure if the induced morphism α−1(O×
Xét) →O×
Xét
is an isomorphism.
A log adic space is a triple (X, MX, α) consisting of an étale sheafy adic space X and a log
structure (MX, α) as above.
A morphism f : (X, MX, αX) →(Y, MY , αY ) of log adic spaces is a morphism f : X →Y of
underlying adic spaces X and Y with a morphism of sheaves of monoids f ♯: f−1MY →MX such

We call such a log structure (MX, α) a log structure on Xét (or simply on X). We omit α if it
is not important or is clear in the context.
Definition 2.2.
(1) Let M be a log structure on Xét. We say MX is integral (resp. saturated)
if it is a sheaf of integral (resp. saturated) monoids. Let f : X →Y be a morphism of adic
spaces.
The inverse image f ∗MY of a log structure MY of Y is defined to be the log

Definition 2.3 ([DLLZ23a, Def. 2.3.1]). Let P be a monoid. A chart of (X, MX) modeled on P
is a morphism θ : PX →MX of étale sheaves of monoids such that α ◦θ factors through O+
Xét and
the associated log structure of α ◦θ is canonically isomorphic to that of α. We say the chart is fs if
P is fs.
A log adic space X is fs (resp. fine) if X étale locally admits fs (resp. fine) charts. Following the
conventions in [Kat89], for a prelog ring/space (A, P), one can associate a log ring/space denoted
by (A, P)a (see also [DLLZ23a, Def. 2.2.2(6)]); the functor (−)a is the left adjoint of the natural
inclusion of the category of log adic spaces into the category of prelog adic spaces.

(1) an fs log adic space over Spa Zp;
(2) an fs log scheme over Spec Zp;
(3) an fs log formal scheme over Spf Zp.
As a (pre-)adic space might not be étale sheafy in general, sometimes it is worth separating the last

(1) A uniquely p-divisible monoid P is called p-finitely generated if P is n-unique for all
positive integers n, and there is a finite set of elements S ⊂P such that the set of all pi-th

(3) A perfectoid log structure (MY , α) on Yτ is p-coherent (resp. p-weakly coherent) if, for
any geometric point x of Yét, there is an étale neighborhood Ux such that MY |Ux admits a
p-finitely generated (resp. p-weakly finitely generated) chart PUx (of log structures on (Ux)τ).
(4) A log structure (MY , α) on Yτ is called fine perfectoid (resp. strongly fine perfectoid) if
MY is integral, perfectoid, and p-weakly coherent (resp. p-coherent).
Remark 2.7. A log structure M on Yv is fine perfectoid implies that ν∗M on Yét is fine perfectoid.
In fact, to see that ν∗M is perfectoid, note that ν∗M ∼= ν∗M and ν∗M♭∼= ν∗M♭; this follows from
Lemma 2.25. Then the isomorphism of sheaves holds after pushforward.
Remark 2.8. We remark that the integrality is crucial in the proof of Theorem 2.18, while perfec-
toidness and (weak) p-finite generation will be used in the proof of Theorem 2.49.
We discuss some basic properties of the log structures defined above.

Let us also remark that there is another way of defining a log diamond, which is a generalization
of [SW20, Def. 8.3.1].
Remark 2.32. Let FSPerf be the category of saturated and fine perfectoid log perfectoid spaces of
characteristic p. We then define a log diamond X as a pro-étale sheaf on FSPerf such that: (1)
There is a surjective map between sheaves Y →X, where Y is representable by an object in FSPerf;
(2) the fiber product of sheaves Y ×X Y is also representable by an object in FSPerf, denoted by
Y ×sat
X Y , such that the two projections p1, p2 : Y ×sat
X Y →Y are (non-log) pro-étale.
We expect that Definition 2.13 should satisfy this definition when X is analytic over Spa Zp (cf.
Lemma 2.47). We do not explore this direction in the current paper.
One can also change the definition of log diamonds by changing the category FSPerf to other
categories with a different class of log structures. This definition may also be viewed as an analogue
of the definition of a log algebraic space in the second sense due to Kajiwara-Kato-Nakayama (see
[KKN15, 10.1]) in perfectoid geometry. The definition of log diamonds in [KY25, Def. 7.3] may also
be viewed as an analogue of log algebraic spaces in the first sense in [KKN15] for some category of
log perfectoid spaces.

2.3. Equivalence of categories on generic fiber. We discuss a generalization of [PR24, §2.5] in
log geometry.
2.3.1.
To formulate the equivalence of categories, we need a notion of (pro-)p-Kummer étale local
systems (see [IKY26])2.
Let (X, MX) be a locally Noetherian fs log adic space. Denote by Loc(Xkét) the category of
Kummer étale local systems on X with values in finite sets. If we fix a finite set or finite abelian
group Λ, we denote by Λ-Loc(Xkét) the category of Kummer étale local systems on X with values
in Λ. Let ζ be a log geometric point on X defined as in [DLLZ23a, 4.4.2-4.4.3]. Let πkét
1 (X, ζ) be
the Kummer étale fundamental group at ζ. The following result is a logarithmic analogue of the
classical theory that étale local systems correspond to finite étale covers.

Zp(GK) by [Yao23]. One can read off the monodromy

E on Breuil-Kisin/Hyodo–Kato log prisms. If we evaluate E at the cover (OK, ϖN) →(OC, ϖN[ 1
p]),
one obtains a Breuil-Kisin-Fargues module (see [Yao23, Ex. 3.1]). Let S = Spa(R, R+) ∈Perf.

fine perfectoid; thus MS♯is uniquely p-divisible (see Lemma 2.10). In particular, under the equiv-
alence of categories between perfect log prisms and perfectoid log rings in [KY25, Prop. 2.39], one
only sees the perfect objects in (OK, ϖN)⋄. Therefore, the realization functor from log prismatic
F-crystals to log shtukas forgets the monodromy action when we consider the specialization map as
in [PR24, Prop. 2.4.6].

3. Local systems and shtukas on mixed Shimura varieties
In this section, we work with the theory in characteristic 0.
3.1. Group-theoretic lemmas. We collect the results used later regarding various group-theoretic
constructions in compactification theory, Bruhat-Tits theory, and their intersection.
3.1.1.
Let P be any linear algebraic group. Define P c := P/Z(P)ac, where Z(P)ac is the anti-
cuspidal part of the multiplicative connected center Z(P)◦of P.
Given a mixed Shimura datum (P, X), let G be the Levi quotient of P.
Then the natural
projection P →G induces an embedding Z(P) ,→Z(G), and Z(P)ac is sent to Z(G)ac by definition.
Note that P c →Gc factors through the Levi quotient G′,c of P c, and G′,c →Gc is a homomorphism
with central kernel.
Lemma 3.1. For a mixed Shimura datum (P, X), we have G′,c = Gc.
Proof. Recall that (P, X) satisfies [Pin90, 2.1 (viii)]. That is, we require that, for any Levi subgroup
eG ⊂P lifting G, the adjoint action of the center Z( eG) of eG on Lie W factors through a cuspidal
quotient; W denotes the unipotent radical of P. Note that the definition of Z( eG) is independent of
the choice of lifting eG. From this condition, we see that
Z(G)ac
∼
−→Z( eG)ac ⊂CentZ( eG)(Lie W) = Z(P).
So the embedding Z(P)ac ,→Z(G)ac admits a section by the displayed expression above. We then
have Z(P)ac = Z(G)ac, and the lemma follows.
□

3.2. Mixed Shimura varieties. In this subsection, we quickly recall some facts about mixed
Shimura varieties.
Let (P, X) be a mixed Shimura datum in the sense of [Pin90]. According to the weight filtration,
there is a tower of mixed Shimura data (P, X) →(P, X) →(Gh, Xh), where Gh is the Levi quotient
of P and P = P/U, where U is a normal subgroup contained in the unipotent radical of P.
Let K be a neat open compact subgroup of P(Af). Define K (resp. Kh) as the image of K in
P(Af) (resp. Ph(Af)). Thus, we have morphisms between mixed Shimura varieties ShK(P, X) →
ShK(P, X) →ShKh(Gh, Xh). The reflex fields of these mixed Shimura varieties are the same.
If we assume that (P, X) = (PΦ, DΦ) comes from a cusp label representative Φ ∈CLR(G, X) of
a Shimura datum (G, X), we call such mixed Shimura data/varieties the boundary mixed Shimura
data/varieties. See [Wu25, §1.1.3]. In this case, U is the center of the unipotent radical of P. Note
that there exist mixed Shimura varieties that do not arise in this manner.
Write the cusp label representative Φ as (QΦ, X+

We say PK := PK(P, X) is de Rham if, for any such pair (ρ, WZp), Lρ,WQp is de Rham. It suffices
to check this on a single faithful representation of P c(Qp). This is independent of the choice of
WZp ⊂WQp.
Proposition 3.14 (Liu-Zhu). The pro-étale torsor PK under Pc(Zp) over Shad
K is de Rham.
Proof. By the rigidity theorem in [LZ17, Thm. 1.1], it suffices to show that, on every geometrically
connected component of ShK, there exists a closed point where the torsor is de Rham. In [Pin90, Def.
11.5], for each morphism of mixed Shimura data ι : (T, Y ) →(P, X) (which is also called a special
point when (T, Y ) is a pure Shimura datum such that T is a torus embedded into P) and for KT ⊂
T(Af), K ⊂P(Af) such that ι(KT ) ⊂K, the induced morphism ShKT (T, Y )C →ShK(P, X)C
descends to a map between canonical models ShKT (T, Y ) →ShK(P, X) ×E(P,X) E(T, Y ). Then
[LZ17, Lem. 4.4] implies that Lρ,WQp is de Rham at special points. Note that, although the notion
of special points for mixed Shimura varieties is slightly generalized in the sense that (T, Y ) can be
a finite cover of a usual Shimura datum, the argument in loc. cit. goes through verbatim since
it only involves class field theory. Since we have a special point on each geometrically connected
component by [Pin90, Lem.
11.6] and the transitivity of the action of P(Af) on geometrically
connected components, the proposition follows.
□

3.4. Torus action on the tower. We re-organize the materials in [Pin90, 3.13-3.16] and [MP19,
2.1.11], and describe the torus action in an explicit and functorial way. This exposition is helpful
in §3.4.3, where we compute the torus action on a tower of mixed Shimura varieties.
Throughout the subsection, we pick any mixed Shimura datum (P, X) and denote its reflex field
by E(P, X); the symbol E denotes its completion at a place v over p.
Sometimes we use the
shorthand E := E(P, X).
3.4.1.
Let U ∼= Gn

Lemma 3.28. Let E0, E1 and E2 be split tori of the same rank over a field k of characteristic zero.
Let f : E1 →E0 and g : E2 →E0 be isogenies between tori. Assume that g is a p-power isogeny.
Then the identity component E3 of E1 ×f,E0,g E2 is a split torus equipped with a p-power isogeny
E3 →E1.
Proof. This fiber product is smooth, so irreducible components are exactly the connected compo-
nents. If we know that E3 is a split torus, then E3 →E1 is a p-power isogeny by construction. We
show that E3 is a split torus. Write the character groups of Ei by Xi for i = 0, 1, 2. Then there are
inclusions X0 ⊂X1 ⊂X0 ⊗Q and X0 ⊂X2 ⊂X0 ⊗Q. Let X′ = X1 ∩X2 ⊃X0 be the intersection
of the lattices X1 and X2 in X0⊗Q. Let X be the abelian group generated by X1 and X2 in X0⊗Q.
We have a map X1⊕X′ X2 →X given by addition in X0⊗Q. This is surjective by the definition of
X and is injective by the definition of X′. Hence, we see that E := Spec k[X] →Spec k[X1]×Spec k[X′]
Spec k[X2] is an isomorphism. On the other hand, the map E →Spec k[X1]×Spec k[X0] Spec k[X2] is
a closed embedding by the separatedness of Spec k[X′] →Spec k[X0], and is a morphism between
varieties of the same dimension. We then conclude that E ∼= E3 := (E1 ×E0 E2)◦.
□

## Page 67

Lemma 3.33. Let A0, A1, A2 be abelian varieties over a field of characteristic 0, and let f : A1 →A0
and g : A2 →A0 be isogenies. Assume that g is a p-power isogeny. Then the identity component
A3 of A1 ×f,A0,g A2 is an abelian variety equipped with a p-power isogeny A3 →A1.
Proof. A1 ×f,A0,g A2 is a closed subgroup of A1 × A2, hence projective; its identity component A3
is therefore an abelian variety by Cartier’s theorem since all varieties are defined over a field of
characteristic zero. By construction, A3 →A1 is a p-power isogeny.
□

Recall the following fact:
Lemma 4.7. Given a morphism between Shimura data ι : (P1, X1) →(P2, X2), then ι(W1) ⊂W2
and ι(U1) ⊂U2, and ι induces G1 →G2 and V1 →V2. In particular, the morphism P1 →P2 is
compatible with U1 ⋊G1 →U2 ⋊G2 in the sense of 1.33.
Proof. Let h1 ∈X1, and set h2 = h1 ◦ι ∈X2. By definition, the weight filtration on Lie Pi induced
by AdPi ◦hi is W−1(Lie Pi) = Lie Wi, W−2(Lie Pi) = Lie Ui. By construction, Lie P1 →Lie P2 is
a morphism between mixed Hodge structures, and it is strict; thus it maps Lie W1 to Lie W2 and
Lie U1 to Lie U2.
□

Theorem 5.5 ([FC90], [Lan16], [MP19], and [Wu25]). For abelian-type Shimura data, there are
integral models satisfying Axiom 5.1 for all K at the beginning of §5.1.
Remark 5.6. In §5.2, we work over the generic fiber, so all arguments work without assumptions.
In §5.3, we assume Axiom 5.1 holds for certain compactifications; however, the only condition we will
need is a stratification with formal completions described by toric schemes. In §5.4, we will further
impose Axiom 4.2 to define canonical integral models of compactifications; as we just mentioned, one
does not have to require the stronger conditions in §5.4 to show the canonical extension of shtukas
to integral models of toroidal compactifications.

5.3. Canonical extensions on integral models. We formulate and prove two general extension
results for log shtukas. The techniques we use are consequences of toric charts but not the geometry
of integral models of (open) Shimura varieties; this, in particular, enables us to show the main
theorems in satisfactory generality.
5.3.1. Gluing lemma. The rough idea of the following lemma is to glue the log shtukas using toric
charts. In particular, we do not use the geometry of SK. Let E be the completion of the reflex
field of the Shimura datum (G, X) that we consider.
Lemma 5.15. We still assume that Σ is smooth projective.
Suppose that there is an integral
model S Σ
K satisfying Axiom 5.1 for (G, X, K). (In fact, we only need the assumptions for toroidal
compactifications.)

embedding j : SK ,→S Σ
K induces an open embedding jW : W 0 := SK ×j,S Σ

The following proposition was inspired by [Har89, 4.1.1] (cf. [HZ01, Prop. 1.3.5]).
Theorem 5.19. Let (G, µ) be as in §1.3. With the conventions above, there is a natural equivalence
of categories between
• The category Sht⋄
G,µ,E′∞(X) of G-shtukas (P, ϕP) with one leg bounded by µ on X⋄that are
equipped with equivariant E′
∞-actions;
• The category Sht⋄
G,µ,E′∞(X(σ), Mσ) of log G-shtukas (P(σ), ϕP(σ)) with one leg bounded by
µ on Xlog ⋄that are equipped with equivariant E′
∞-actions.
The morphisms in the two categories are those preserving the E′
∞-actions. In fact, we show that
every object in the first category can be uniquely extended to the second one.
Note that the last statement in the theorem is stronger than saying the restriction functor is
essentially surjective.
We prove this theorem in §5.3.3. An immediate corollary is:

6. Shtukas on integral models of boundary mixed Shimura varieties of abelian
type
In this section, we construct shtukas on integral models of boundary mixed Shimura varieties of
abelian type, and verify the axioms 4.2.
6.1. Hodge-type case.
6.1.1. Set up. Let (G, X) be a Hodge-type Shimura datum, and let G be a stabilizer quasi-parahoric

have a good theory of compactifications as in [MP19] and to ensure good compatibility of levels

by µΦ. As in [PR24, §4.6.3, Step(3)], we lift Spa D to a point ˜x : Spa(C, OC) →([
SKΦ)ad, where C

Proposition 6.21. There always exists a Hodge-type lifting (G, X) such that the assumptions in
Proposition 6.20 are true.
Construction 6.22. For an abelian-type (G2, X2), we associate a Hodge-type (G, X) such that it
satisfies (1) of Proposition 6.20; this can be done by the construction as in [Del79, Prop. 2.3.10]
and [KPZ24] for any prime p. We now modify the Shimura datum to make it to satisfy (2) of
Proposition 6.20.
Let (T, h) ⊂(G, X) be a special point with T a maximal torus. We first consider Gdb := G×Gab G
where the map G →Gab = G/Gder is the natural one. Denote T ab := T/(T ∩Gder). Define a refined
construction Grf := G ×Gab T.
Since (G, X) is of Hodge-type, we can decompose G to an almost-direct product G = Gder·Zc·Gm,
where Zc is an R-anisotropic torus. Then Gdb ∼= (Gder × Gder) · Zc · Gm ⊂G × G.
In the second expression of the above line, Zc · Gm maps to G × G diagonally into (Zc · Gm) ×
(Zc ·Gm). Alternatively, Gdb ∼= ((Gder ×Gder)×Zc ·Gm)/Zc ·Gm ∩Gder, where the last intersection
maps to the first factor diagonally and to the second factor naturally.
Denote the corresponding Shimura data by (Gdb, Xdb) and (Grf, Xrf), respectively. There is a
natural map (Grf, Xrf) →(Gdb, Xdb). Then both of them are of Hodge type because they are both
contained in the Hodge-type Shimura datum defined by G ×η,Gm,η G.

Proposition 6.23. Proposition 6.21 is true for any prime p. Given (G2, X2) of abelian type, the
accessible Hodge-type lifting is (Grf, Xrf), as given by Construction 6.22.
More precisely, π(Grf,der, Grf) is trivial. In particular, the intersection of any parahoric subgroup
˘Kp of Grf(˘Qp) with Grf,der(˘Qp) is parahoric.
Proof. It suffices to show that π(Grf,der, Grf) is trivial. We first consider π(Gdb,der, Gdb). By Lemma
6.24 below, we have that
π(Gdb,der, Gdb) = diag{1,2}{π(Gder, G)},
where the index {1, 2} labels the two factors of Gdb,der = Gder × Gder.
Note that Grf,der = Gder × {1} ⊂Gdb,der. Then π(Gder × {1}, Gdb) ⊂π(Gdb,der, Gdb) is trivial. So
π(Grf,der, Grf) ⊂π(Grf,der, Gdb) is trivial.
□

7. Well-positioned subschemes
Fix a Shimura datum (G, X) and a quasi-parahoric model G of GQp. For each Φ ∈CLR(G, X),

we work under Assumption 5.26.

scheme over OE. A locally closed subset (resp. subscheme) Y of SK,T := (SK)T is called well

Remark 7.8. As explained in [Mao25b, Lem. 2.16, 2.20, Prop. 2.30], the connected components
and closures of Newton strata, as well as N ≤[b] for any [b] ∈B(Gc, {µc,−1}), are all well-positioned.
The same applies to central leaves, KR strata, and EKOR strata proved in later sections, and we do
not repeat the arguments there.
The following proof was explained to us by Sian Nie.
Lemma 7.9. Let G be any reductive group over Qp, M be any proper Levi subgroup of G, µ be a
cocharacter of M that is non-central in G. The image of B(M, {µ}) →B(G, {µ}) does not contain
the basic element of B(G, {µ}).
Proof. Given [b1] ≤[b2] in B(M, {µ}), then [b1] ≤[b2] in B(G, {µ}).
Since basic elements are
minimal, it suffices to show that given the basic element [b] ∈B(M, {µ}), its image in B(G, {µ})
can not be basic.
We fix a pinning and assume µ is dominant in G. Let µ⋄be the σ-average of µ, recall that
B(M, {µ}) = {[b] ∈B(M)| κ([b]) ≤κ(µ), νb ≤µ⋄}
Let [b] ∈B(M, {µ}) be basic, then νb = µ⋄−h is central in M, where h is a non-negative linear
combination of positive coroots of M. Recall that [b] is basic in B(G, {µ}) if and only if νb is
central in G if and only if ⟨α, νb⟩= 0 for all simple roots in G. For those simple roots α not in M,
⟨α, µ⋄⟩≥0 since µ⋄is dominant in G, and ⟨α, h⟩≤0 by the Cartan matrix, then
⟨α, νb⟩= ⟨α, µ⋄⟩−⟨α, h⟩≥0,
it takes equality if and only if ⟨α, µ⋄⟩= ⟨α, h⟩= 0. This implies that h = 0. Since νb = µ⋄is central
in M, then µ⋄is central in G, we get a contradiction.
□
Corollary 7.10. Basic Newton stratum has no boundary.

acter µ is central in G if and only if G = T is a torus, and the Shimura variety associated with
(T, {h}) has no boundary.

Lemma 7.15. Let S be a perfect scheme and let P be a quasi-parahoric group scheme. Given
p : S →ShtW
P →IsocP , we define (the set of k-points of) central leaves to be the fibers of S(k) →

P (k), and define Newton strata to be the fibers of S →B(P). Then Newton strata are locally
closed in S, and central leaves are closed in the (set of k-points of) Newton strata.
Proof. When P is parahoric, this follows from [HK25, Prop. 2.15]. Note that in [HK25, §2], it is
not necessary to assume that the generic fiber of P is reductive; the setup applies to any flat affine
group scheme of finite type over the base with connected fibers. The references [ARH19], [ARH21],
and [IKY24, Thm. A.14] also work in this general setting.
When P is quasi-parahoric, we instead apply the arguments in the proof of Lemma 7.5, using
[DvHKZ26, Prop. 2.3.1] with π0(Pc)ϕ in place of π0(Gc)ϕ.
□

also remark that the existence of integral models at parahoric levels with schematic local model
diagrams is still not known for all abelian-type Shimura data when p = 2; cf. [Yan25] for recent

Appendix A. Some category theory
This appendix serves as a complement to the categorical language employed throughout the
paper, offering a direct reference applicable to the context considered herein.
A.1. Morphisms between presheaves in categories. Let C be a (1-)category. Let Categories
(resp. Groupoids) be the 2-category (resp. (2, 1)-category) of categories (resp. groupoids). Let F1
and F2 be two (2-)functors (or presheaves) F1, F2 : Cop →Categories.
As in [Sta25, Ex. 02XV], there are fibered categories p1 : X1 →C and p2 : X2 →C corresponding
to F1 and F2, respectively. More precisely, a category X with a functor p to C is called a fibered
category if, for any x ∈X with f : V →p(x) ∈MorC(V, p(x)), there exists a strongly Cartesian lift
y →x over f (cf. [Sta25, Def. 02XM]).
A (1-)morphism F : (X1, p1) →(X2, p2) is a 1-morphism in the 2-category of fibered categories
over C. That is, F is a functor from X1 to X2 such that p2 ◦F = p1, and F sends a strongly
Cartesian morphism to a strongly Cartesian morphism (see [Sta25, Def. 02XP]).
On the other hand, we define 1-morphisms between presheaves in categories.
Definition A.1. Define a (1-)morphism (or a natural transformation) F : F1 →F2 to be the
collection {F(U); ϕ(U1, U2, f)}U,U1,U2∈Ob C,f∈MorC(U1,U2) as follows:
(1) For any U ∈Ob C, F(U) : F1(U) →F2(U) is a 1-morphism (i.e., a functor) between
categories.
(2) For f ∈MorC(U1, U2) with U1, U2 ∈Ob C,
ϕ(U1, U2, f) : F(U1)F1(f)(F1(U2)) →F2(f)F(U2)(F1(U2))
is a natural transformation between functors F(U1)F1(f) and F2(f)F(U2) mapping from
F1(U2) to F2(U1) such that,
(a) ϕ(U, U, idU)(x) = idF(U)x for any U ∈Ob C and x ∈F1(U).
(b) For any morphisms in C, f : U1 →U2 and g : U2 →U3, and any x3 ∈Ob F1(U3), we
have F2(f)(ϕ(U2, U3, g)) ◦ϕ(U1, U2, f)(F1(g)x3) = ϕ(U1, U3, g ◦f)(x3).
(c) For any pair (U1, U2) and f : U1 →U2, the diagram
F1(U2)
F1(U1)
F2(U2)
F2(U1)
F1(f)
F(U2)
F(U1)
F2(f)
is commutative up to composing ϕ(U1, U2, f).
In fact, the definitions of 1-morphisms in the two contexts correspond to each other.
Lemma A.2. F determines and is determined by a morphism F from F1 to F2.
Proof. Recall that the fibered category p1 : X1 →C is defined as follows: the objects are (U, x)
such that U ∈Ob C and x ∈Ob F1(U); the morphisms are MorX1((U1, x1), (U2, x2)) = {(f, ϕ)|f ∈
MorC(U1, U2), ϕ ∈MorF1(U1)(x1, F1(f)x2)}. The composition is defined as (g, ψ) ◦(f, ϕ) = (g ◦
f, F1(f)(ψ) ◦ϕ).


## Page 119


119
The 1-morphism F maps (U, x) to (V, y) ∈Ob X2. Since p2 ◦F = p1, we have that V = U and
y ∈Ob F2(U). This determines an assignment F(U) from Ob F1(U) to Ob F2(U).
Hence, the functor F maps MorX1((U1, x1), (U2, x2)) to MorX2((U1, F(U1)x1), (U2, F(U2)x2)).
More precisely, a pair (f, ϕ) as above is sent to (F(f), F(ϕ)). But F(f) = f since p2 ◦F = p1. So
F(ϕ) : F(U1)x1 →F2(f)(F(U2)x2) is a uniquely determined morphism in F2(U1) induced by F.
Now let (U1, x1) = (U1, F1(f)x2) and let ϕ = id. Then F(ϕ) =: ϕF(U1, U2, f)(x2) is a functor

Conversely, suppose that there is a 1-morphism F : F1 →F2 given by the collection {F(U); ϕ(U1, U2, f)}.
